ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u247	That is how, and now let us move forward in the debate. First Minister of Labour Harakka from his own field of work 5 minutes and then Minister Kulmuni also 5 minutes. After that, the debate will continue on those subjects.
ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u248	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen! The Government aims to enable a socially, economically and ecologically sustainable society. We have wrapped up the sleeves and made a swift move in our actions in the work for the welfare of the nation, and we are well on the road to reform. We have agreed on a number of measures to improve economic growth and employment development. Many of these are reflected in the draft budget for 2020. Economic growth has been good in recent years, which has boosted positive employment developments. Next year the employment rate is expected to reach 73.2 per cent, while the unemployment rate will fall to 6.3 per cent. With the government's active measures, we are climbing step by step towards the 75% employment rate we have set. To achieve this goal, we need to be better able to integrate part-time, harder-to-work young people, older people and people with immigrant backgrounds into the labour market. We need a wide range of measures, and our actions must be effective, and that is what they are. In the budget debate, we set out a number of measures to promote employment, which are being prepared by me. I'm going over the list here. We will reform the pay subsidy in a broad sense, increase its use and remove bottlenecks. In fact, these are three pay subsidies: general wage subsidies, wage subsidies for organisations and new self-employed workers. Existing wage subsidies will become wider, more attractive and easier. The conditions for company pay support are better and clearer. Getting it is simpler and more secure. We are looking at the possibilities of increasing the maximum wage subsidy and a special increase for disabled people. We will introduce a processing time guarantee in electronic services, and even now, as early as this year, we will support employers by providing training for work to help with paid work more widely. We have also added employees to the payment of pay subsidies, so it is worth taking this opportunity already this year. Thus, the general wage subsidy is intended for jobseekers who are at risk of prolonged unemployment, including part-time and older jobseekers who experience a lack of knowledge and age discrimination. The second wage subsidy, the wage subsidy for organisations, is aimed at the most difficult people to employ. It's a step towards working life. It is essential that both pay subsidies are linked to the development of skills. At the end of the cycle, we need to see how knowledge has grown. Entrepreneurship is important to this government and to me personally. That is why we are launching a recruitment test to promote fast employment and lower the threshold for self-employed and small micro-enterprises to hire additional labour. Legislation on all three forms of support is already under way, with the aim of bringing government proposals to Parliament already in spring 2020 and into force in the autumn. Another major reform that is already under way concerns the trials of municipalities in employment services, to which I invited municipalities to participate by letter yesterday Tuesday. The pooling of municipal and state resources will increase customer orientation and, above all, improve employment among those in a weak labour market position. In pilot and collaborative cities, the average period of unemployment has already been significantly lower than in the control regions. Here, too, we will act quickly: we will do our best to bring the government's proposals to this Chamber again this year, and the municipal trials will start already in the spring. In other words, and repeat: the government has not waited until next year, but is already taking action. Anyone who claims otherwise will speak the truth that has been deliberately altered. Mr President! Next year's budget includes a number of other actions that will lead to Finland, which is inclusive and competent according to the title of the Government Programme. We put in place a disability programme for those who are able to work part-time, through which we make it easier for those who are able to work part-time to find employment. We promote the employment of immigrants outside the labour force and in a weak labour market position by expanding the multi-professional centres of excellence of immigrants. We also invest more in guidance and counselling centres. In order to strengthen the availability of skilled labour and improve labour matching, we will launch a comprehensive international recruitment programme. Under my leadership, we will also set up a team of researchers to explore ways of promoting employment. And, of course, we will reverse the active model from the beginning of next year. In addition to this busy list, we also outlined a number of other measures to improve employment in the budget debate. And as far as the draft budget for 2020 is concerned, it includes sufficient appropriations for the new employment policy measures and reforms that we have decided on. I am prepared to describe them in more detail during the start-up debut.
ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u249	And Minister Kulmuni, including five minutes.
ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u250	Mr President! The Government Programme is based on the objectives of sustainable growth. This requires business renewal, growth and strong internationalisation. For the Ministry of Employment and the Economy, this means, among other things, a strong investment in entrepreneurship, supporting new investments, promoting exports, industrial competitiveness, increasing renewable energy and working against climate change. Regional policy also has an important role to play here. Mr President! The Ministry of Employment and the Economy invests in research, development and innovation activities and internationalisation. Business Finland's grant funding will be increased by EUR 70 million compared to the current year's budget. The direction of R & D funding will be reversed and the rate towards the 4% GDP target will be reversed. Entrepreneurship plays a crucial role in the growth objectives. Our task is to build a favourable environment for investment and internationalisation and to ensure that companies have access to skilled labour and that there are no thresholds for hiring it. By the end of the year, an entrepreneurship strategy will be drawn up and its comprehensive implementation will have to start at the beginning of next year. The aim is to diversify the business structure and encourage growth, especially for SMEs. Finland has rightly been concerned about the relatively low level of investment. To this end, the current 50% increase in guarantees for Finnvera SMEs will be investigated. Other parts of the guarantee scheme will also be gutted so that investments can be speeded up better. At the same time, we also want to lower the threshold for self-employed entrepreneurs, micro-entrepreneurs, to hire additional labour by launching a recruitment test for SMEs next year, and to ensure that skilled workers are available at all times. The shortage of beneficiaries is currently the single largest obstacle to growth in many regions, and this issue is being addressed. The International Growth Programme will be launched next year. It is very important for a small export-driven country. EUR 16.5 million has also been earmarked for this. During the whole parliamentary term, more than EUR 90 million will be allocated to the whole. The programme will improve the efficiency of residence permit processes and launch international recruitment policies, increase resources to attract international experts and launch a growth programme for SMEs. It is absolutely essential for Finland to have international experts to support and enable growth. As far as climate change is concerned, although it is one of the greatest challenges of our time, it also offers opportunities if we consider how Finland can offer the world the best possible technology, new solutions both nationally and internationally. Strong know-how in technology, its foresight, of course, is absolutely central to us. In this context, for example, energy efficiency, new technologies and the shift in energy production from fossil to renewable and decentralised energy production, next year's budget will include increases in low carbon and circular economy. Finland will continue its work to give up coal. This phase-out will be encouraged over the next three years by an additional mandate of EUR 90 million, the first of which will be EUR 30 million as early as next year. Therefore, the objective is to enable and mobilise these compensatory investments by 25 C.E. The circular economy is heavily invested. The total budget for next year is set at 25 million appropriations, not only for innovation but also for investment programmes in the circular economy. At the same time, a national biogas programme has also been set up and its implementation will be set at EUR 5 million per year for the next two years. The aim is not only to increase decentralised biogas production but also to expand the distribution network so that biogas is a real alternative to more and more motorists and also to heavy-duty drivers. Mr President! Finally, a few words on regional policy and regional development. The aim is to reduce regional segregation. Finland must differ but not be unequaled. In practice, this means supporting the strengths of cities, municipalities, regions themselves, increasing contractualism. There is also an additional amount of EUR 20 million in the proposal, and that is more than ever before. Regional transport aid will continue. It is a very important tool for long distances in Finland and also an important tool for economic and regional policy. Here, Mr President, is a brief overview of the budgetary issues that I am responsible for.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u357	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen of Parliament! Antti Rinte's new red mud government makes record investments in Finnish transport infrastructure during the Government's term of office. For next year, we will propose a total budget of around EUR 3.5 billion for the administrative sector of the Ministry of Transport and Communications. This is more than EUR 600 million more than in the current year's budget. We will improve the transport network, reduce the debt to repair and increase the funding for public transport. Already in the June amending budget, the Government decided to launch a number of development and basic bus maintenance projects. From the beginning of next year, a permanent increase of EUR 300 million will be made in basic infrastructure maintenance, of which EUR 20 million will be allocated to winter maintenance. This controls and gradually dismantles the repair debt. Together with other increases, the financing of basic bus maintenance will increase by a total of EUR 362 million in 2020. As part of raising funding for basic transport infrastructure management, a comprehensive package of small improvement projects will be implemented at regional level to promote conditions for walking and cycling, improve road safety and ensure the smooth operation of business transport. Mr President! The development of a national transport system plan is the most important and most important transport policy action of this regime. The 12-year super-government transport system plan will ensure the long-term sustainability and coherence of transport policy. One of the key reasons why Finland has not managed to obtain EU funding for the development of the transport network is precisely the lack of long-term preparation. Now this is going to change. A parliamentary working party will be set up today to guide the preparation of the plan, and representatives of all parliamentary groups will be invited. I would therefore like to stress that the long-term development of Finnish transport policy requires good cooperation between all parties. Mr President! The Government is making a responsible climate policy. We want Finland to be carbon neutral in 2035 and we are the first fossil-free welfare society in the world. This also requires a significant reduction in transport emissions. Our objective is to halve transport emissions by 2030 and we will draw up a road map for the transition to fossil-free transport. As part of the reduction in emissions from transport, we will show a record total of almost EUR 25 million for walking and cycling in 2020. A permanent climate-based increase of EUR 20 million will be made in public transport. Mr President! The government is investing in rail transport. For our first job in the supplementary budget for the summer, we decided to start major rail projects on the main track, on the Turku shore track, on the railway yards of Joensuu and Oulu, between Kouvola and Kotka-Hamiina and between Iisalmi-Ylivieska. We are also committed to developing fast future rail links. The Government is prepared to finance the planning of the Turku hour train and the Finnish railway line by a total of EUR 115 million if an agreement is reached with municipalities on railway projects. With regard to the Eastern Rapid Connection, there will be a readiness to take decisions in spring 2020 when the policy review and comparison are completed. In accordance with the Government Programme, we will also improve the rail connections connected to the main railway line between Tampere-Pori, Tampere-Jyväskylä and Seinäjoki-Vasa. We also electrify the track between Hanko and Hyvinkää after decades of waiting. Mr President! Improving road safety is one of the government's key priorities. We will prepare a road safety resolution, we will propose EUR 12 million in additional funding next year to eliminate dangerous level crossings, we will secure funding for transport security and we will target funding for basic infrastructure maintenance on road safety-enhancing targets. We will also increase the funding of private roads. A total of EUR 20 million will be allocated to private road grants in 2020. Mr President! The Meteorological Institute is conducting a state-of-the-art study that we as a nation can be proud of. An increase of EUR 700000 is proposed to the Finnish Meteorological Institute to set up the ICAO Global Space Weather Centre and an increase of EUR 600000 is proposed to the Finnish Meteorological Institute to finance the operation of the European Small Particle, Cloud and Low Gas Research Infrastructure. Finally, I would like to highlight the very necessary additional funding for Finland's cyber security. An additional EUR 1.2 million will be proposed to the Cyber Security Centre, and we will also devote EUR 2 million to the coordination and improvement of cyber security. Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen! The Finnish people voted for the change in the elections, and the government's budget for next year is the first step of this change.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u418	Thank you, Minister Marin. Then we move to the Ministry of the Interior, Minister Ohio, five minutes.
ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u419	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen of Parliament! The Government Programme aims to secure the rule of law Finland, to strengthen the sense of security and to ensure the functioning of security authorities. Everyone in Finland must be able to count on help and security when it is most needed. This draft budget focuses on Finland's continuing to remain one of the safest countries in the world. Among other things, the government will raise the number of policemen to 7500 by the end of 22. A total of EUR 816 million is proposed for the police action. In 2020, an increase in the number of policemen per year will be launched in line with the Government Programme to the level of 7500, to which EUR 7.5 million is proposed in the budget. Strengthening police resources can further improve the results of police activities and the security situation in society and contribute to preventive action. The time frame for police intervention will be improved and more and more citizens will receive urgent assistance faster than before. The activities of police patrols can be targeted in particular at those regions with the lowest levels of service at the moment. The Government also aims to prevent sexual offences against children and to strengthen the prevention and prevention of environmental crimes in accordance with the Government Programme. In crime against children and young people in particular, the strengthening of cybercrime investigations plays a key role. In addition to caring for the safety of Finns, it is important to ensure that fundamental rights are implemented. The strengthening of civil intelligence and the judicial supervision of the protection police is reflected in this budget. The Government proposes an appropriation of EUR 380000 for the 2020 budget and an additional EUR 190000 for the 21 and 22 envelopes to strengthen civilian intelligence guidance and the law enforcement of the protection police. The Government sees close guidance and supervision of civilian intelligence activities as particularly important in order to ensure their relevance and relevance and the implementation of fundamental rights. The Government also strengthens border security and ensures the implementation of Finland’s international obligations. A total of EUR 267.84 million is proposed to the Border Guard in the budget for 20 years, which is more than EUR 24 million more than in the actual budget for 2019. Finland is also committed to the activities of the European Border and Coast Guard Frontex and the establishment of a permanent force. During the spending limits period, more than EUR 4 million will be allocated annually to the permanent European Border and Coast Guard. Technical control systems will also improve border management. The draft budget for the year 20 includes a total of EUR 15 million in compensation for investments in border technical control systems of the Border Guard. The reform of existing systems will reduce the need to replace obsolete technologies with additional staff. The safety of Finnish everyday life is also promoted by increasing the training and recruitment of emergency services. A total of EUR 74.3 million is proposed for the operating expenditure of the emergency services and the Emergency Response Centre, which is more than EUR 4 million more than in the actual budget of 19. The shortage of on-call personnel in emergency centres is reflected in the rapid availability of assistance to citizens and the division of on-call staff at work. With these additional contributions, we will correct this problem. The fairness and legal security of asylum processing will be promoted by investing in the resources of the Finnish Immigration Service. The budget of the Finnish Immigration Service will be increased by EUR 10 million in the 2020 budget and the 2021 budget framework to ensure a high-quality, smooth and legal certainty regarding asylum. This appropriation is intended to cover expenditure relating to the implementation of the budget of the European Agricultural Guidance and Guarantee Fund (EAGGF). It is in everyone's interest that the asylum procedure should be conducted properly and smoothly from the outset in order to reduce waiting times and reduce the need for redress. The number of asylum applications received mainly during the years 15 and 16 remains untenable. The Government reserves EUR 3 million for the years 20 and 21 in order to get more pairs of hands out of traffic and complete the processing. The length of processing times also has a direct impact on the amount of reception costs, so the additional resources allocated by the government to support decisions and to moderate processing times are not only a humane but also an economically sound solution. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u484	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen! This government will increase the well-being of people. The draft budget will improve the livelihood of the most vulnerable. This can be seen, among other things, in the increase in basic security, pensions receiving a small pension, the single-parent distribution of child supplements and maintenance allowance. The full amount of the national pension will be increased by EUR 34 and the full amount of the guarantee pension by EUR 50 per month. It has been more than ten years since the previous increase in the national pension level. The cost of raising pensions to public finances is EUR 183 million net. The basic security, i.e. daily unemployment benefit and labour market support, minimum rehabilitation allowance and sickness and parental allowance, will be increased by EUR 20. The cost of this is about EUR 40 million. The child supplement for single parents and the child allowance for the fourth and subsequent children will increase by EUR 10. At the same time, the increase in the basic part of the single parent's income support will be increased by around EUR 20 so that single-parent families who receive income support will benefit from the increase in the child's allowance. The maintenance allowance will be increased by EUR 7. A total of over EUR 36 million has been earmarked for these reforms. In addition, index increases are normally made in the benefits. Mr President! These investments in social security will reduce poverty. As a result of changes in the budget as a whole, low incomes are estimated to fall by around 0.3 percentage points to 13% and the risk of poverty is eliminated by around 18,000 people. An estimated 3800 households would eliminate the need to apply for income support. I am particularly pleased that this government is dismantling the unfair active model. This will also help to improve the basic security of the unemployed, as the operating cutter of the active model will no longer reduce the amount of compensation for anyone in the future. We are also launching a wider social security reform in the autumn, and it aims at a clear and effective social security from a human perspective, which guarantees sufficient basic security in all life situations. The reform relates to the coordination of work and social security, uniform concepts, levels and payment periods of benefits and the improvement of services covered by social security. This social security reform will be carried out in the Parliamentary Committee together with all the parliamentary parties. Additional resources have been allocated to the Ministry for the preparation of the social security reform and the second major reform of the STM, the social security reform, for next year amounting to EUR 1.2 million. The Government’s goal is to have the best working life in the world, where everyone’s knowledge and skills can be used. The achievement of the objectives of employment and well-being at work will be supported in the budget of the Ministry of Social Affairs and Health through, among other things, the part-time disability programme, the Work Life and Well-being Programme and the continuation of the funding of pay support from unemployment benefit appropriations. A total of EUR 10 million will be allocated to the work capacity programme for the part-time disabled to be implemented together with the Ministry of Employment and the Economy for 2020. The Ministry of Social Affairs and Health accounts for EUR 4.5 million for 2020. An amount of EUR 1 million has been set aside for the start of the programme for the development of working life and well-being at work for 2020. The administrative sector of STM in the state budget proposal for 2020 amounts to EUR 14.8 billion, which is just over a quarter of the state budget. The draft budget is EUR 256 million less than the actual budget for 2019. The fall in expenditure is mainly due to an increase in employment and a reduction in the reduction in the employer's health insurance contribution. Mr President! Krista Kiuru, Minister of Family and Basic Services, will continue to present the budget of the Ministry of Social Affairs and Health.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u486	Mr President! As Mr Pekonen has already said, our section accounts for EUR 14.8 billion, or about a quarter, of the state budget, and it specifically contributes to the objective of social sustainability — a really big and important objective that is also being achieved in this content. The aim is therefore to strengthen the whole package of social security, which consists of services and adequate income security, but ultimately, in particular, of the government's decision to invest in people. Mr President! The budget of the Ministry of Social Affairs and Health therefore supports a larger government policy based on the idea of the welfare economy. The goal is to support people’s well-being and to strengthen and secure the conditions for a good life for all. We believe that the economy will also be strengthened by investing in policies, institutions and structures which, in the short and long term, create the conditions for well-being, good life and a sustainable society. Well-being people are the foundation of a sustainable and prosperous economy, both as workers, as experts and as members of our community. The aim of the service system must be to promote and maintain the well-being, health, functional and working capacity of the population, social security and reduce differences in well-being and health. For some of our population, these objectives are already being fulfilled, but some of them would be different from the actual use of services, and the available service does not always meet their needs, so there is plenty to be said about, especially since, according to the Finnish Constitution, everyone should have the right to adequate social and health services. In this Chamber, the message of Article 19 of the Constitution has become famous in recent years. It has even been seen as an obstacle to the development of social and health services, at least as a delay. We are leaving the government from a different starting point. This budget is precisely proof that this article obliges us to develop social and health services and also to allocate more money to these services. In fact, the development of social and health care has, as has been said, focused heavily on administrative structures. The importance of the basic level has been emphasised, but at the same time the basic services have been cut, and it certainly has not increased people's confidence in social reform. With our own efforts, we will seek to restore this confidence and to implement social and health care reform as both a substantive and a structural project as of next year. This is how, in particular, the reform of social security as a substantive reform will start this time by strengthening basic services at the beginning of next year. Social and health care services will therefore be improved by, for example, improving the provision of care guarantees and the minimum size of care staff for older people. In general, EUR 97 million will be allocated to the development and improvement of social and health services in order to strengthen future social welfare centres, which will also be aimed at radiating into all the key areas where there are areas for improvement. Among other things, it is good to list these from the mental health point of view. As I said, care for the elderly will be systematically developed in such a way as to systematically address and provide better solutions. It should also be noted that more state support is required for specialist training in occupational health care and that funding is also provided for the central government contributions to basic municipal services, both for the implementation of the extensive emergency services at Vaasa Central Hospital and also for the support of the diversity of families and life situations, and even for the appointment of the High Commissioner for the Elderly, which has long been expected. One of the central objectives of the Government Programme has been to strengthen the services of children and families, which means that there will be a continuation of the programme for the change of child and family services, funding for the preparation of the child strategy, more funding for the operation of shelters and the care of women using drugs, as well as for children's services.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u732	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen, please. First of all, from the general section number 28, where the aim is therefore the best public administration in the world, in which the state administration operates in accordance with all principles of good governance: it means citizen-centredness and reliability. The Ministry of Finance is responsible for the effectiveness and efficiency of public administration as part of the Government. Digitalisation is what is invested in to improve public services and customer orientation. Digitalization also develops multi-spatiality and flexible work, which is important for climate change, of course, but also for the realisation of equality. The municipalities will receive significant support as they provide important basic services, and the central government share and tax compensation of basic services will be 1.1 billion more in 20 municipalities than this year’s level, of which 237 million will be paid this year. Thus, the time for index increases and reductions is running out, and the level of basic municipal services will rise from 7.1 billion in 2020 to 7.4 billion in the spending limits period. Then a few words about these in particular. The Ministry of Finance has a productivity allocation of around EUR 3 million and is used specifically for the acquisition and implementation of investments, studies, studies and training aimed at promoting productivity. This does not reduce public staff. This is not a staff cut, it is a genuine productivity cut. EUR 38 million will be invested in digitalisation over the next year, which will ensure high-quality and accessible digital services and will guide them so that Finland will continue to be the number one category in the digitalisation of public administration in the future. These services will be used by individual persons or companies in conjunction with public activities. With regard to security authorities, approximately EUR 8 million will be invested in communications networks, including, of course, the use, maintenance of public communications services based on the safety radio network and the development of a new broadband public service. In support of municipalities, to which I have just referred, the central government contribution to basic services and the equalisation of the state contribution based on tax revenues are considered important and is able to maintain basic services everywhere. These include the extension of subjective early childhood education and the reduction of group sizes, the extension of Vaasa Hospital to a large emergency hospital, changes in the diversity of family life situations — a total of EUR 36 million for all these new tasks. The changes in the tax bases will be compensated for and paid for in the future by a separate item which will appear to all of you who have been familiar with this book in the past, now different because they have previously been on one item. These allowances amount to EUR 2.2 billion. Thus, the central government share of municipalities in the provision of basic services rises to 25.46 per cent. The index increase in basic services, which has thus been frozen, now rises by 2.4 per cent, i.e. an increase of EUR 166 million. The revision of cost distribution between central government and municipalities will increase the central government share of basic services by EUR 102 million. The changes in double taxation in the Kiky agreement, i.e. the holiday allowance cut, will therefore be compensated for this year, i.e. the EUR 237 million. And then this side of the Prime Minister's Office, that is, dividend income and share sales income. After all, the state is, in principle, a strong owner in terms of social and business terms, making long-term ownership so that we have returns and a response to social services. According to the budget book, dividend income and share sales are estimated to be around EUR 2 billion, of which EUR 750 million are reserved for future government investments, and this EUR 750 million is the sum that will in principle have to be covered by the sale of assets, i.e. dividend income is sufficient for this other share. — Thank you very much, this is very brief.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u791	Your Honor! Ladies and gentlemen of Parliament! This government is committed to combating climate change and halting the loss of biodiversity. The objective of carbon-neutral Finland recorded in the Government Programme in 2035 is a key indicator of the Government's success in its policy. The Ministry of the Environment has an important role to play in moving forward the Government's ambitious climate policy. Krista Mikkonen, Minister for the Environment and Climate, is the chairman of the Ministerial Group on Climate and Energy Policy. The ministerial working group is responsible for coordinating and coordinating the Government's climate policy activities. These activities are carried out in the administrative branches of several ministries. In this work, the ministerial group will be assisted by a climate panel, whose funding will now be strengthened. The goal of carbon-neutral Finland is not easy to achieve, and it is not possible to change everything in an instant. Many climate measures are already underway in the draft budget, but most of the work is still ahead of us. Next year, there will be the preparation of the Climate Act, the reduction of environmentally and climate-friendly business subsidies and the overall reform of energy taxation, which will also assess the tax treatment of peat. In the draft budget, the Government supports the reduction of emissions from housing with the support of new energy efficiency renovations. It is directed specifically at housing companies and has a budget of EUR 20 million for the year 20. This will boost energy renovations and help create new jobs and jobs. Aid for the abandonment of oil heating will start in 21 C.E. We will speed up the electrification of transport by investing in increasing charging points. The support for the charging infrastructure for electric cars is more than tripled compared to the current year. The construction of wooden blocks of flats is supported by an increased start-up grant of 20 % for wood-based ARAs in the MAL areas. Housing policy also helps to respond to the ageing of the population. The Government supports the renovation of housing to be suitable and accessible for older people by EUR 5 million in 20. An action programme for older people's housing will also be drawn up for the period 20-22. Elevator and accessibility allowances will be continued with increased allocations. The Government's draft budget is strongly in favour of Finnish nature. A further EUR 100 million will be proposed for nature conservation next year. With this, we want to protect the nature that is dear to Finns, the vibrant forests and the sound of music. In addition to protection, weakened nature around Finland will also be restored and restored. Our objective is to halt the loss of biodiversity by 30. Additional funding will make a significant contribution to this objective. Increasing the funding of nature conservation is an important act for Finland’s future. The loss of biodiversity is a threat comparable to climate change, and that is why the government is now taking strong action. Already one in nine species in Finland and even half of the habitats are endangered. Additional funding for nature conservation will be used, inter alia, to increase the number of protected areas. The Government's investments in forest protection will reach the target of 96,000 hectares of the Metso Programme by 25. The Government also invests in improving weakened habitats with a ten-year programme. Within the framework of this habitat programme, the state of nature that is dear to Finns will be improved. On a voluntary basis, the Government will launch a further proposal on the protection of protected areas. In addition, ditched bogs, birdwaters and wetlands and traditional environments such as meadows, meadows, fellings and forest pastures will be restored and refurbished, as well as improved coastal and aquatic conditions. Natural friends should also be pleased that this government is investing in improving the state of popular national parks. The repair debt of hiking structures, such as longwoods and trails, is reduced in order to ensure that people travel on the right routes and not unnecessarily consume sensitive nature. In the future, Metsähallitus' nature services will also do more restoration and rehabilitation work in our protected areas. The Government also aims to halve homelessness during this term of government. To this end, a development programme of EUR 3 million will be launched in 20 and will run until 2022. - Thank you.

And the openings related to peat have also been based on this programme last season. It is a good thing that we have now clarified what the government's view is about the continued use of peat. It has become clear that the government has no intention of interfering with peat burning, tax subsidies for peat burning. This entry in the Government Programme for halving peat combustion by 2030 will happen without any change to this separate support that exists for peat burning regardless of its climate and environmental impacts. Another aspect of peat is what the government programme says nothing about, that is, the continued use of peat in general. The use of peat is not decreasing on the basis of government policies. On the contrary, new uses are being developed for peat, as was heard in this statement by the chairman of the Committee on the Environment. So with regard to the use of peat, I don't think there's going to be any change for the government. Especially for colleagues from the centre, who were involved in the government last season to put Finland in order — as the city centre's election slogan was. The growth in debt had just been halted. It had not yet been brought to a terrible halt, but it had been brought to an end. Public debt fell temporarily below the 60% threshold. And now the government debt is turning back to growth. A direct quote from the government budget for next year: ‘The economy is growing withthelargest potential at its speed and without prejudice to the increase in expenditure, it exceeds revenue growth. “ So this was directly from the government budget. And let me point out that the draft budget for next year is indeed EUR 2 billion in deficit. This means that there will be a significant increase in debt, debt will be reversed and taxes will also be raised. We've been talking about a reduction in income tax. But the fact is that in the future the employee will be less left in the hands of his own salary.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u859	Mr President! I am speaking in plain language because I am speaking in plain language. According to a recent estimate by the Selkokeskus, up to 150,000 Finns find it difficult to understand the common language. For example, immigrants, mentally disabled persons, memory-disadvantaged people and young people who have barriers to reading can be included in the group. Many people would like to work, but the language causes problems. It'll help with the plain language. The English language is Finnish or Swedish, which has been adapted in terms of vocabulary, structure and content to make the general language easier. The language has been used in Finland for over 30 years, but now it is used more. Finland's employment rate is set to rise to 75%. Whom does plain language help to find employment, and how? Language causes problems at different stages of job search for both the jobseeker and the employer. A job seeker may wonder: I can't find any information on an open job. I don't understand a job ad. I don't know how to fill out a job application and its attachments. I know my job, but I can't make a convincing CV. I know how to treat old people, but I don't know how to write a treatment plan. The employer may wonder: Why can't I get answers to a job ad? When a job seeker can't describe his own skills, can he do anything? Many texts are too difficult. It's worth writing them in plain language. Mr President! I propose that we make more extensive use of plain language as we increase employment in our country and implement a government programme. I suggest that we organise an English course here in Parliament as well.

ParlaMint-FI_2019-10-09-ps-47.u863	Mr President! Neither the centre nor the government is afraid to use the word regional policy. It is social equality and justice. Every corner of the house is equally valuable. Minister Kulmunia is grateful for the fact that he has launched a study on how to increase work in places independent and multi-spatial — the ways in which working, living and working in many places is possible. Many Finns live in everyday life, where the home region, the roots of man, are at the heart of his life. The aim of the new regional policy is to give people freedom and the opportunity to choose where to build their lives. It is based on the strengths of different parts of the country. It is great that Finland is different, but it must not be different. Finland needs to be developed on an equal footing and listening to people. We defend the freedom of people to choose their place of residence from the end of remote gravel roads as well as from a dense city centre. We want to develop well-functioning transport links to ensure the smooth movement of industry, trade, goods and people throughout the country. Roads to remote villages as well as to the city centre are just as much worth developing. Mr President! The Government’s goal is that every province has a university and that every child and young person can study as close to their home as possible. Access to education has a direct impact on the level of education of the young person. The proximity of educational institutions is also very important for regional enterprises, both in terms of the availability of labour and the vitality of the regions. Indeed, Minister Kosonen was determined to fight for additional funding for higher education institutions during the budgetary negotiations. Further funding is very important to advance towards the goal of the education policy vision and to raise the educational level of Finns. The Government is taking a step towards a better Finland. The Centre supports all the employment-promoting solutions agreed in the budget debate, but it is obvious that a lot more decisions are also needed. Seven concrete proposals have been put forward by the Centre to promote employment. In summary, they are: Let us not undermine the cost-competitiveness that last season was hard to build. Promoting local reconciliation — in particular the obstacles to local reconciliation enshrined in the law must be dismantled. The Finnish model is prepared, where the employee, when receiving the increased basic security, also undertakes to accept work from outside his or her profession and neighbourhood. This is a clear adjunct to the problem of retrieval. Support the employment of part-time workers through tailor-made support. The employment of part-time workers is relatively low in Finland. The Central Parliamentary Group is also ready to facilitate the availability of labour from outside the EU and the EEA. Invest in education, product development and research and remove obstacles to investment. A programme of its own is needed to employ young people. I would also courageously use private employment services to ensure that the operator also has a clear responsibility for results in terms of the employment of young people. These measures will enable us to get on a good journey and speed up the government's path towards the employment objective.

This morning a competitiveness comparison was published, which was global. Finland was in its eleventh position, so to speak. The reason for this was that Finland is a stable country and that Finland has stable institutions, i.e. the courage to invest here. Trust is also needed for people, and that is what we have wanted in this budget. We need effective health services, we need social security, we need effective childcare, we need confidence that the family will have the courage to base, we need education services and, above all, we need jobs. This is the big equation that comes from the fact that we have work, we have livelihoods, we companies trust, and that is how we can also provide those services. Mr. Speaker! There are a number of details within this budget, some of which I would like to highlight, first of all, infra. EUR 300 million is invested in basic infrastructure management, which is more than necessary. In southern Ostrobothnia, the Ely Centre shouted an emergency call last week: several hundred kilometres of the picnic road is threatened to be a gravel road. The business community needs these roads, they need investments. When this EUR 300 million is distributed, I really hope that it will go to the whole country, so that, first and foremost, funds will be available for these necessary projects. Secondly, rails: It is good to invest in the rails and encourage people on the rails. It is an environmentally friendly and good way, a quick way to move from one place to another. The main track gets the attention it deserves in many ways, but I also pay attention to the cross - link that is needed in this country. The cross-border connections serve the less populated countryside in particular, and one of the most important is the cross-border connection between Jyväskylä, Seinäjoki and Tampere, the Haapamäki railway. This is where train shifts will increase from the beginning of next year, and this development must of course continue. There are many tourist destinations, it is needed for pendeling, people's mobility and it is also needed for freight transport. The second important transversal link is Highway 18, which must be strongly promoted during this parliamentary term. [Petri Honkonen’s interlude]

This is what we did in Sipilä's government, when the taxation of labour was reduced by 1.4 billion, with a focus on low and middle-income employees, with a focus on low and middle-income employees. Labour taxation is still very high in Finland, and tax reform by reducing labour taxation and shifting taxation to indirect taxation is a good structural policy that supports the objective that more hours and more work are worthwhile in all situations. We have continued to propose reducing the tax burden on labour and collecting tax revenue from harmful taxes and indirect taxes. Representative Ovaska, net of zero — net of zero. We all know that it is easy to increase expenditure. Of course it is. It is therefore much more difficult to make decisions that raise employment and permanently bring about expenditure that can be used to finance these good things. And, as I said yesterday, the Ministry of Finance has not been able to assess the positive impact on employment for any of the employment targets proposed by the government. On the contrary, economists estimate that your budget will reduce employment by up to 16,000 people. So, ladies and gentlemen, your aim is getting further and further away, and this is a fact that you will not escape. Of course, you understand that you are nervous, because ordinary drivers will remember you when these increases get along. Firstly, you have decided to increase fuel taxation by 250 million next year. It will be visible in the gas pumps in August and will not have a real impact on emissions either. In its own proposal, the Government itself states that in the short term this tax increase is expected to reduce the consumption of diesel-driven passenger cars by 1.4% — that is, 1.4% — and the tax burden on the motorist continues to increase. Another issue of surprise, which we have also touched upon again today, relates to the government's decision to abolish the tax subsidy on paraffinic diesel, totalling EUR 120 million. When transport emissions can be reduced precisely with cleaner fuels and a newer fleet of cars, this decision is really taking you and Finland in the wrong direction. It vaccinates the purest diesel with emissions up to 90% lower than fossil diesel emissions. So, yes, Mr President, we must ask where is the logic of this government in terms of transport and climate policy. Because these decisions certainly do not show that. I ask the government one thing: see if it is possible to retain renewable paraffinic diesel under this tax subsidy, even if the fossil paraffinic would be cut out of the tax subsidy.
